None of it is ever really urgent, but I just keep leaving my seat.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or nervous right before you get up from your seat in these situations?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's not anxiety or nervousness. I just... I just feel this urge to move. It's like I can't stay put, so I just get up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this urge to get up and move only in situations like meetings or dinners, or do you feel it at other times as well?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely at other times, too. Actually, since the house got quieter after my youngest left for college, I've noticed I just... keep going. I'll move straight from lesson planning to doing the laundry, then I'm off to softball practice, or maybe chopping food for a community event. I just do it all without much of a pause. Even on the nights I promise myself I'm just going to sit down and rest, I still end up moving from one thing to the next.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're trying to avoid thinking about something or feeling a certain way when you keep getting up and moving from one activity to another?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I'm not really trying to avoid anything, like bad thoughts or feelings. I'm not feeling sad or stressed or anything like that. I just... I just have a lot of energy, I guess. It's just how I am. I don't think there's any hidden reason behind it, I just don't like sitting still for too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these actions, like getting up or moving to another task, in a specific or repetitive way?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. There's no routine to it, or... or like a specific way I have to do things. I just move on to whatever catches my eye or whatever needs getting done next. It's not a habit I have to repeat, just me keeping busy.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted by your surroundings or other things going on around you?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I don't really get distracted by things around me. When I'm doing a task, I'm focused on it until I'm ready to move on to the next thing. It's more about just needing to be active, rather than losing my train of thought or getting pulled away by what's going on in the room.\n\nDoctor: Has your difficulty staying seated or constant need to be active caused any problems for you at work, at home, or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, it actually has. It's started to affect my relationships, both at work and out in my community. During staff meetings at the school, or even just at my local book club... people start to lose patience with me. They get annoyed that I can't just sit quietly like everyone else. So, instead of feeling connected to my colleagues or my friends, I usually just leave feeling embarrassed by it all.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about not being able to sit still or control your need to be active?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel guilty about it. I mean, I feel embarrassed when people get annoyed with me, like I mentioned, but guilt? Not really. It's just how I am, and I'm not doing it to hurt anyone. I just... I just have a lot of energy and need to move. So, no, guilt isn't really the word I'd use.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice that you had trouble staying seated or felt this constant need to be active?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, it's been my whole life. Even as a little girl. I remember looking at my old report cards from elementary school... the teachers would always write that I was a bright student, but that I just could not stay in my seat or wait my turn. My mother actually used to joke about it. She'd say that I had worn a path in the floor between my desk and the pencil sharpener before I was even ten years old. So, it's really always been this way for me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It's more about just needing to be active, rather than losing my train of thought or getting pulled away by what's going on in the room.\n\nDoctor: Has your difficulty staying seated or constant need to be active caused any problems for you at work, at home, or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, it actually has. It's started to affect my relationships, both at work and out in my community. During staff meetings at the school, or even just at my local book club... people start to lose patience with me. They get annoyed that I can't just sit quietly like everyone else. So, instead of feeling connected to my colleagues or my friends, I usually just leave feeling embarrassed by it all.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about not being able to sit still or control your need to be active?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel guilty about it. I mean, I feel embarrassed when people get annoyed with me, like I mentioned, but guilt? Not really. It's just how I am, and I'm not doing it to hurt anyone. I just... I just have a lot of energy and need to move. So, no, guilt isn't really the word I'd use.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice that you had trouble staying seated or felt this constant need to be active?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, it's been my whole life. Even as a little girl. I remember looking at my old report cards from elementary school... the teachers would always write that I was a bright student, but that I just could not stay in my seat or wait my turn. My mother actually used to joke about it. She'd say that I had worn a path in the floor between my desk and the pencil sharpener before I was even ten years old. So, it's really always been this way for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to constantly be active and move has significantly impacted your daily life or overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not really sure if I'd say it's significantly impacted my overall well-being. I mean, yes, like I said, it causes some embarrassment in meetings and things like that, but... I still manage to do my job, and I still have my life. It's more of a frustration than something that completely derails my day-to-day living. I just kind of work around it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Actually, since the house got quieter after my youngest left for college, I've noticed I just... keep going. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thank you. Well, what brought me in is that I keep getting up in situations where I'm supposed to stay seated. Like during staff meetings at the school, or even just at dinner. Everyone else will be sitting there, and I'll suddenly feel the need to get up and refill my coffee, or check a handout, or just walk to the back of the room. None of it is ever really urgent, but I just keep leaving my seat.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or nervous right before you get up from your seat in these situations?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's not anxiety or nervousness. I just... I just feel this urge to move. It's like I can't stay put, so I just get up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this urge to get up and move only in situations like meetings or dinners, or do you feel it at other times as well?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely at other times, too. Actually, since the house got quieter after my youngest left for college, I've noticed I just... keep going. I'll move straight from lesson planning to doing the laundry, then I'm off to softball practice, or maybe chopping food for a community event. I just do it all without much of a pause. Even on the nights I promise myself I'm just going to sit down and rest, I still end up moving from one thing to the next.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're trying to avoid thinking about something or feeling a certain way when you keep getting up and moving from one activity to another?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I don't really get distracted by things around me. When I'm doing a task, I'm focused on it until I'm ready to move on to the next thing.     "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thank you. Well, what brought me in is that I keep getting up in situations where I'm supposed to stay seated. Like during staff meetings at the school, or even just at dinner. Everyone else will be sitting there, and I'll suddenly feel the need to get up and refill my coffee, or check a handout, or just walk to the back of the room. None of it is ever really urgent, but I just keep leaving my seat.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or nervous right before you get up from your seat in these situations?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's not anxiety or nervousness. I just... I just feel this urge to move. It's like I can't stay put, so I just get up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this urge to get up and move only in situations like meetings or dinners, or do you feel it at other times as well?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely at other times, too. Actually, since the house got quieter after my youngest left for college, I've noticed I just... keep going. I'll move straight from lesson planning to doing the laundry, then I'm off to softball practice, or maybe chopping food for a community event. I just do it all without much of a pause. Even on the nights I promise myself I'm just going to sit down and rest, I still end up moving from one thing to the next.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're trying to avoid thinking about something or feeling a certain way when you keep getting up and moving from one activity to another?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I'm not really trying to avoid anything, like bad thoughts or feelings. I'm not feeling sad or stressed or anything like that. I just... I just have a lot of energy, I guess. It's just how I am. I don't think there's any hidden reason behind it, I just don't like sitting still for too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these actions, like getting up or moving to another task, in a specific or repetitive way?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. There's no routine to it, or... or like a specific way I have to do things. I just move on to whatever catches my eye or whatever needs getting done next. It's not a habit I have to repeat, just me keeping busy.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted by your surroundings or other things going on around you?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I don't really get distracted by things around me. When I'm doing a task, I'm focused on it until I'm ready to move on to the next thing. It's more about just needing to be active, rather than losing my train of thought or getting pulled away by what's going on in the room.\n\nDoctor: Has your difficulty staying seated or constant need to be active caused any problems for you at work, at home, or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, it actually has. It's started to affect my relationships, both at work and out in my community. During staff meetings at the school, or even just at my local book club... people start to lose patience with me. They get annoyed that I can't just sit quietly like everyone else. So, instead of feeling connected to my colleagues or my friends, I usually just leave feeling embarrassed by it all.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about not being able to sit still or control your need to be active?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel guilty about it. I mean, I feel embarrassed when people get annoyed with me, like I mentioned, but guilt? Not really. It's just how I am, and I'm not doing it to hurt anyone. I just... I just have a lot of energy and need to move. So, no, guilt isn't really the word I'd use.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice that you had trouble staying seated or felt this constant need to be active?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, it's been my whole life. Even as a little girl. I remember looking at my old report cards from elementary school... the teachers would always write that I was a bright student, but that I just could not stay in my seat or wait my turn. My mother actually used to joke about it. She'd say that I had worn a path in the floor between my desk and the pencil sharpener before I was even ten years old. So, it's really always been this way for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to constantly be active and move has significantly impacted your daily life or overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not really sure if I'd say it's significantly impacted my overall well-being. I mean, yes, like I said, it causes some embarrassment in meetings and things like that, but... I still manage to do my job, and I still have my life. It's more of a frustration than something that completely derails my day-to-day living. I just kind of work around it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your inability to stay seated and constant need to be active is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Well, distress might be a strong word, but... it is definitely affecting my relationships. Both at work and in my community. \n\nPeople just lose patience with me. It happens in staff meetings at the school, or even at my book club. I'll get up or shift around too much, and I can tell they get annoyed. And then I end up leaving those gatherings feeling embarrassed instead of, you know, actually feeling connected to the people there. So it is certainly upsetting in that sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to take time off work or significantly adjust your daily responsibilities because of your inability to stay seated or constant need to be active?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't ever had to take time off for it. I've never missed work or had to, you know, step away from my normal duties. Honestly, being a teacher actually helps a bit because I don't have to be tied to a desk all day—I can walk around the classroom or pace a little while I'm talking to the students. So, no, I haven't had to significantly change my life or drop any responsibilities. I just keep doing what I need to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your need to be active and move has interfered with your ability to complete tasks or achieve your goals?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I still get everything done that I need to do. I finished my graduate degree years ago, and I always get my lesson plans and grading done on time for my classes. \n\nI might be, you know, shifting around in my chair or pacing around the living room while I do those things, but the work still gets finished. Like during staff meetings at the school, or even just at dinner. Everyone else will be sitting there, and I'll suddenly feel the need to get up and refill my coffee, or check a handout, or just walk to the back of the room. None of it is ever really urgent, but I just keep leaving my seat.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or nervous right before you get up from your seat in these situations?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's not anxiety or nervousness. I just... I just feel this urge to move. It's like I can't stay put, so I just get up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this urge to get up and move only in situations like meetings or dinners, or do you feel it at other times as well?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely at other times, too. Actually, since the house got quieter after my youngest left for college, I've noticed I just... keep going. I'll move straight from lesson planning to doing the laundry, then I'm off to softball practice, or maybe chopping food for a community event. I just do it all without much of a pause. Even on the nights I promise myself I'm just going to sit down and rest, I still end up moving from one thing to the next.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're trying to avoid thinking about something or feeling a certain way when you keep getting up and moving from one activity to another?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I'm not really trying to avoid anything, like bad thoughts or feelings. I'm not feeling sad or stressed or anything like that. I just... I just have a lot of energy, I guess. It's just how I am. I don't think there's any hidden reason behind it, I just don't like sitting still for too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these actions, like getting up or moving to another task, in a specific or repetitive way?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. There's no routine to it, or... or like a specific way I have to do things. I just move on to whatever catches my eye or whatever needs getting done next. It's not a habit I have to repeat, just me keeping busy.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted by your surroundings or other things going on around you?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I don't really get distracted by things around me. When I'm doing a task, I'm focused on it until I'm ready to move on to the next thing. It's more about just needing to be active, rather than losing my train of thought or getting pulled away by what's going on in the room.\n\nDoctor: Has your difficulty staying seated or constant need to be active caused any problems for you at work, at home, or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, it actually has. It's started to affect my relationships, both at work and out in my community. During staff meetings at the school, or even just at my local book club... people start to lose patience with me. They get annoyed that I can't just sit quietly like everyone else. So, instead of feeling connected to my colleagues or my friends, I usually just leave feeling embarrassed by it all.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about not being able to sit still or control your need to be active?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel guilty about it. I mean, I feel embarrassed when people get annoyed with me, like I mentioned, but guilt? Not really. It's just how I am, and I'm not doing it to hurt anyone. I just... I just have a lot of energy and need to move. So, no, guilt isn't really the word I'd use.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice that you had trouble staying seated or felt this constant need to be active?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, it's been my whole life. Even as a little girl. I remember looking at my old report cards from elementary school... the teachers would always write that I was a bright student, but that I just could not stay in my seat or wait my turn. My mother actually used to joke about it. She'd say that I had worn a path in the floor between my desk and the pencil sharpener before I was even ten years old. So, it's really always been this way for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to constantly be active and move has significantly impacted your daily life or overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not really sure if I'd say it's significantly impacted my overall well-being. I mean, yes, like I said, it causes some embarrassment in meetings and things like that, but... I still manage to do my job, and I still have my life. It's more of a frustration than something that completely derails my day-to-day living. I just kind of work around it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your inability to stay seated and constant need to be active is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Well, distress might be a strong word, but... it is definitely affecting my relationships. Both at work and in my community. \n\nPeople just lose patience with me. It happens in staff meetings at the school, or even at my book club. I'll get up or shift around too much, and I can tell they get annoyed. And then I end up leaving those gatherings feeling embarrassed instead of, you know, actually feeling connected to the people there. So it is certainly upsetting in that sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to take time off work or significantly adjust your daily responsibilities because of your inability to stay seated or constant need to be active?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't ever had to take time off for it. I've never missed work or had to, you know, step away from my normal duties. Honestly, being a teacher actually helps a bit because I don't have to be tied to a desk all day—I can walk around the classroom or pace a little while I'm talking to the students. So, no, I haven't had to significantly change my life or drop any responsibilities. I just keep doing what I need to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your need to be active and move has interfered with your ability to complete tasks or achieve your goals?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I still get everything done that I need to do. I finished my graduate degree years ago, and I always get my lesson plans and grading done on time for my classes. \n\nI might be, you know, shifting around in my chair or pacing around the living room while I do those things, but the work still gets finished. Actually, since the house got quieter after my youngest left for college, I've noticed I just... keep going. I'll move straight from lesson planning to doing the laundry, then I'm off to softball practice, or maybe chopping food for a community event. I just do it all without much of a pause. Even on the nights I promise myself I'm just going to sit down and rest, I still end up moving from one thing to the next.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're trying to avoid thinking about something or feeling a certain way when you keep getting up and moving from one activity to another?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I'm not really trying to avoid anything, like bad thoughts or feelings. I'm not feeling sad or stressed or anything like that. I just... I just have a lot of energy, I guess. It's just how I am. I don't think there's any hidden reason behind it, I just don't like sitting still for too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these actions, like getting up or moving to another task, in a specific or repetitive way?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. There's no routine to it, or... or like a specific way I have to do things. I just move on to whatever catches my eye or whatever needs getting done next. It's not a habit I have to repeat, just me keeping busy.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted by your surroundings or other things going on around you?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I don't really get distracted by things around me. When I'm doing a task, I'm focused on it until I'm ready to move on to the next thing. It's more about just needing to be active, rather than losing my train of thought or getting pulled away by what's going on in the room.\n\nDoctor: Has your difficulty staying seated or constant need to be active caused any problems for you at work, at home, or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, it actually has. It's started to affect my relationships, both at work and out in my community. During staff meetings at the school, or even just at my local book club... people start to lose patience with me. They get annoyed that I can't just sit quietly like everyone else. So, instead of feeling connected to my colleagues or my friends, I usually just leave feeling embarrassed by it all.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about not being able to sit still or control your need to be active?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel guilty about it. I mean, I feel embarrassed when people get annoyed with me, like I mentioned, but guilt? Not really. It's just how I am, and I'm not doing it to hurt anyone. I just... I just have a lot of energy and need to move. So, no, guilt isn't really the word I'd use.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice that you had trouble staying seated or felt this constant need to be active?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, it's been my whole life. Even as a little girl. I remember looking at my old report cards from elementary school... the teachers would always write that I was a bright student, but that I just could not stay in my seat or wait my turn. My mother actually used to joke about it. She'd say that I had worn a path in the floor between my desk and the pencil sharpener before I was even ten years old. So, it's really always been this way for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to constantly be active and move has significantly impacted your daily life or overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not really sure if I'd say it's significantly impacted my overall well-being. I mean, yes, like I said, it causes some embarrassment in meetings and things like that, but... I still manage to do my job, and I still have my life. It's more of a frustration than something that completely derails my day-to-day living. I just kind of work around it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your inability to stay seated and constant need to be active is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Well, distress might be a strong word, but... it is definitely affecting my relationships. Both at work and in my community. \n\nPeople just lose patience with me. It happens in staff meetings at the school, or even at my book club. I'll get up or shift around too much, and I can tell they get annoyed. And then I end up leaving those gatherings feeling embarrassed instead of, you know, actually feeling connected to the people there. So it is certainly upsetting in that sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to take time off work or significantly adjust your daily responsibilities because of your inability to stay seated or constant need to be active?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't ever had to take time off for it. I've never missed work or had to, you know, step away from my normal duties. Honestly, being a teacher actually helps a bit because I don't have to be tied to a desk all day—I can walk around the classroom or pace a little while I'm talking to the students. So, no, I haven't had to significantly change my life or drop any responsibilities. I just keep doing what I need to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your need to be active and move has interfered with your ability to complete tasks or achieve your goals?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I still get everything done that I need to do. I finished my graduate degree years ago, and I always get my lesson plans and grading done on time for my classes. \n\nI might be, you know, shifting around in my chair or pacing around the living room while I do those things, but the work still gets finished. So, no, it hasn't really stopped me from completing my tasks or reaching my goals. I just might be a bit restless while I'm doing them.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been unable to participate in social activities or occupational tasks because of your need to be active and move?\n\nPatient: No, it's never actually stopped me from participating in anything. Um, I still go to everything I'm supposed to. Like I mentioned, I still attend my staff meetings and my book club, and I obviously teach my classes every day. So, no, I've never had to skip an activity or a work task just because I feel restless. I just go ahead and do it, even if I have to shift around while I'm there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to constantly be active and move is affecting your performance or relationships at work in a major way?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it is affecting my relationships, actually. They get annoyed with all my shifting and pacing. So instead of feeling connected to my colleagues or my friends, I just end up leaving those gatherings feeling embarrassed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your inability to stay seated and constant need to be active has significantly impacted your ability to function effectively in your role as a teacher?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say it affects my actual teaching. When I'm in the classroom, being on my feet and moving around is really just part of the job. I can walk up and down the aisles, write on the board, and check on the students, so it's not out of the ordinary to be active. \n\nSo, no, it hasn't impacted my ability to function effectively with the kids or do my daily work. It's really just those times when I'm expected to sit quietly, like the staff meetings I mentioned, where it becomes an issue. But the teaching itself is fine.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It's like I can't stay put, so I just get up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this urge to get up and move only in situations like meetings or dinners, or do you feel it at other times as well?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely at other times, too. Actually, since the house got quieter after my youngest left for college, I've noticed I just... keep going. I'll move straight from lesson planning to doing the laundry, then I'm off to softball practice, or maybe chopping food for a community event. I just do it all without much of a pause. Even on the nights I promise myself I'm just going to sit down and rest, I still end up moving from one thing to the next.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're trying to avoid thinking about something or feeling a certain way when you keep getting up and moving from one activity to another?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I'm not really trying to avoid anything, like bad thoughts or feelings. I'm not feeling sad or stressed or anything like that. I just... I just have a lot of energy, I guess. It's just how I am. I don't think there's any hidden reason behind it, I just don't like sitting still for too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these actions, like getting up or moving to another task, in a specific or repetitive way?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. There's no routine to it, or... or like a specific way I have to do things. I just move on to whatever catches my eye or whatever needs getting done next. It's not a habit I have to repeat, just me keeping busy.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted by your surroundings or other things going on around you?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I don't really get distracted by things around me. When I'm doing a task, I'm focused on it until I'm ready to move on to the next thing. It's more about just needing to be active, rather than losing my train of thought or getting pulled away by what's going on in the room.\n\nDoctor: Has your difficulty staying seated or constant need to be active caused any problems for you at work, at home, or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, it actually has. It's started to affect my relationships, both at work and out in my community. During staff meetings at the school, or even just at my local book club... people start to lose patience with me. They get annoyed that I can't just sit quietly like everyone else. So, instead of feeling connected to my colleagues or my friends, I usually just leave feeling embarrassed by it all.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about not being able to sit still or control your need to be active?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel guilty about it. I mean, I feel embarrassed when people get annoyed with me, like I mentioned, but guilt? Not really. It's just how I am, and I'm not doing it to hurt anyone. I just... I just have a lot of energy and need to move. So, no, guilt isn't really the word I'd use.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice that you had trouble staying seated or felt this constant need to be active?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, it's been my whole life. Even as a little girl. I remember looking at my old report cards from elementary school... the teachers would always write that I was a bright student, but that I just could not stay in my seat or wait my turn. My mother actually used to joke about it. She'd say that I had worn a path in the floor between my desk and the pencil sharpener before I was even ten years old. So, it's really always been this way for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to constantly be active and move has significantly impacted your daily life or overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not really sure if I'd say it's significantly impacted my overall well-being. I mean, yes, like I said, it causes some embarrassment in meetings and things like that, but... I still manage to do my job, and I still have my life. It's more of a frustration than something that completely derails my day-to-day living. I just kind of work around it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your inability to stay seated and constant need to be active is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Well, distress might be a strong word, but... it is definitely affecting my relationships. Both at work and in my community. \n\nPeople just lose patience with me. It happens in staff meetings at the school, or even at my book club. I'll get up or shift around too much, and I can tell they get annoyed. And then I end up leaving those gatherings feeling embarrassed instead of, you know, actually feeling connected to the people there. So it is certainly upsetting in that sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to take time off work or significantly adjust your daily responsibilities because of your inability to stay seated or constant need to be active?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't ever had to take time off for it. I've never missed work or had to, you know, step away from my normal duties. Honestly, being a teacher actually helps a bit because I don't have to be tied to a desk all day—I can walk around the classroom or pace a little while I'm talking to the students. So, no, I haven't had to significantly change my life or drop any responsibilities. I just keep doing what I need to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your need to be active and move has interfered with your ability to complete tasks or achieve your goals?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I still get everything done that I need to do. I finished my graduate degree years ago, and I always get my lesson plans and grading done on time for my classes. \n\nI might be, you know, shifting around in my chair or pacing around the living room while I do those things, but the work still gets finished. So, no, it hasn't really stopped me from completing my tasks or reaching my goals. I just might be a bit restless while I'm doing them.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been unable to participate in social activities or occupational tasks because of your need to be active and move?\n\nPatient: No, it's never actually stopped me from participating in anything. Um, I still go to everything I'm supposed to. Like I mentioned, I still attend my staff meetings and my book club, and I obviously teach my classes every day. So, no, I've never had to skip an activity or a work task just because I feel restless. I just go ahead and do it, even if I have to shift around while I'm there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to constantly be active and move is affecting your performance or relationships at work in a major way?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it is affecting my relationships, actually. It's... it's started to cause some issues at work and in my community. People just lose patience with me when I can't sit still during staff meetings or at my book club. They get annoyed with all my shifting and pacing. So instead of feeling connected to my colleagues or my friends, I just end up leaving those gatherings feeling embarrassed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your inability to stay seated and constant need to be active has significantly impacted your ability to function effectively in your role as a teacher?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say it affects my actual teaching. When I'm in the classroom, being on my feet and moving around is really just part of the job. I can walk up and down the aisles, write on the board, and check on the students, so it's not out of the ordinary to be active. \n\nSo, no, it hasn't impacted my ability to function effectively with the kids or do my daily work. It's really just those times when I'm expected to sit quietly, like the staff meetings I mentioned, where it becomes an issue. But the teaching itself is fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to constantly be active and move is currently causing problems for you in more than one area of your life, such as at work and in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'd say it is. Like I was saying before, it's definitely affecting my relationships both at work and in my personal life. At work, my colleagues are losing patience with me during staff meetings because I can't just sit still. And it's the same thing in my community, like with my book club. People get annoyed with me, and instead of feeling connected to my friends or coworkers, I just end up leaving those situations feeling really embarrassed. So, yes, it's causing problems in both of those areas.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Well, I don't know if I would use the word severely, especially since I can still teach just fine. But... like I was saying earlier, it definitely impacts my quality of life when it comes to my relationships. It really just comes back to people losing patience with me at work meetings and at my book club. So it is a problem for me, even if it isn't completely ruining my life.

It's not anxiety or nervousness. I just... I just feel this urge to move. It's like I can't stay put, so I just get up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this urge to get up and move only in situations like meetings or dinners, or do you feel it at other times as well?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely at other times, too. Actually, since the house got quieter after my youngest left for college, I've noticed I just... keep going. I'm not really trying to avoid anything, like bad thoughts or feelings. I'm not feeling sad or stressed or anything like that. I just... I just have a lot of energy, I guess. It's just how I am. I don't think there's any hidden reason behind it, I just don't like sitting still for too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these actions, like getting up or moving to another task, in a specific or repetitive way?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. There's no routine to it, or... or like a specific way I have to do things. I just move on to whatever catches my eye or whatever needs getting done next. It's not a habit I have to repeat, just me keeping busy.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted by your surroundings or other things going on around you?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I don't really get distracted by things around me. When I'm doing a task, I'm focused on it until I'm ready to move on to the next thing. It's more about just needing to be active, rather than losing my train of thought or getting pulled away by what's going on in the room.\n\nDoctor: Has your difficulty staying seated or constant need to be active caused any problems for you at work, at home, or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, it actually has. It's started to affect my relationships, both at work and out in my community. During staff meetings at the school, or even just at my local book club... people start to lose patience with me. They get annoyed that I can't just sit quietly like everyone else. So, instead of feeling connected to my colleagues or my friends, I usually just leave feeling embarrassed by it all.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about not being able to sit still or control your need to be active?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel guilty about it. I mean, I feel embarrassed when people get annoyed with me, like I mentioned, but guilt? Not really. It's just how I am, and I'm not doing it to hurt anyone. I just... I just have a lot of energy and need to move. So, no, guilt isn't really the word I'd use.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice that you had trouble staying seated or felt this constant need to be active?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, it's been my whole life. Even as a little girl. I remember looking at my old report cards from elementary school... the teachers would always write that I was a bright student, but that I just could not stay in my seat or wait my turn. My mother actually used to joke about it. She'd say that I had worn a path in the floor between my desk and the pencil sharpener before I was even ten years old. So, it's really always been this way for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to constantly be active and move has significantly impacted your daily life or overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not really sure if I'd say it's significantly impacted my overall well-being. I mean, yes, like I said, it causes some embarrassment in meetings and things like that, but... I still manage to do my job, and I still have my life. It's more of a frustration than something that completely derails my day-to-day living. I've never missed work or had to, you know, step away from my normal duties. Honestly, being a teacher actually helps a bit because I don't have to be tied to a desk all day—I can walk around the classroom or pace a little while I'm talking to the students. So, no, I haven't had to significantly change my life or drop any responsibilities. I just keep doing what I need to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your need to be active and move has interfered with your ability to complete tasks or achieve your goals?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I still get everything done that I need to do. So, no, it hasn't really stopped me from completing my tasks or reaching my goals. I just might be a bit restless while I'm doing them.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been unable to participate in social activities or occupational tasks because of your need to be active and move?\n\nPatient: No, it's never actually stopped me from participating in anything. So it is a problem for me, even if it isn't completely ruining my life.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your need to constantly be active and move has caused significant problems in your ability to maintain healthy relationships or succeed in your job as a teacher?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 60 / secondary_school_teacher / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"It has started to affect my relationships at work and in my community because people lose patience with me in meetings and book club, and I leave feeling embarrassed instead of connected.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, like I've been saying, it hasn't stopped me from succeeding as a teacher—I can still do my job in the classroom just fine. But... yes, it's definitely causing problems with my relationships. It's just getting hard because people are losing patience with me, both at work during our staff meetings and in my community at my book club. Instead of feeling connected to my coworkers or my friends, I just end up leaving those situations feeling really embarrassed. So, yes, it's definitely affecting my relationships.

It's not anxiety or nervousness. I just... I just feel this urge to move. It's like I can't stay put, so I just get up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this urge to get up and move only in situations like meetings or dinners, or do you feel it at other times as well?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely at other times, too. I don't really get distracted by things around me. When I'm doing a task, I'm focused on it until I'm ready to move on to the next thing. It's more about just needing to be active, rather than losing my train of thought or getting pulled away by what's going on in the room.\n\nDoctor: Has your difficulty staying seated or constant need to be active caused any problems for you at work, at home, or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, it actually has. It's started to affect my relationships, both at work and out in my community. During staff meetings at the school, or even just at my local book club... people start to lose patience with me. So, instead of feeling connected to my colleagues or my friends, I usually just leave feeling embarrassed by it all.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about not being able to sit still or control your need to be active?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel guilty about it. I mean, I feel embarrassed when people get annoyed with me, like I mentioned, but guilt? Not really. It's just how I am, and I'm not doing it to hurt anyone. I just... I just have a lot of energy and need to move. So, no, guilt isn't really the word I'd use.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice that you had trouble staying seated or felt this constant need to be active?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, it's been my whole life. My mother actually used to joke about it. She'd say that I had worn a path in the floor between my desk and the pencil sharpener before I was even ten years old. So, it's really always been this way for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to constantly be active and move has significantly impacted your daily life or overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not really sure if I'd say it's significantly impacted my overall well-being. I mean, yes, like I said, it causes some embarrassment in meetings and things like that, but... I still manage to do my job, and I still have my life. It's more of a frustration than something that completely derails my day-to-day living. I just kind of work around it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your inability to stay seated and constant need to be active is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Well, distress might be a strong word, but... it is definitely affecting my relationships. So it is certainly upsetting in that sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to take time off work or significantly adjust your daily responsibilities because of your inability to stay seated or constant need to be active?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't ever had to take time off for it. I've never missed work or had to, you know, step away from my normal duties. Honestly, being a teacher actually helps a bit because I don't have to be tied to a desk all day—I can walk around the classroom or pace a little while I'm talking to the students. So, no, I haven't had to significantly change my life or drop any responsibilities. I just keep doing what I need to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your need to be active and move has interfered with your ability to complete tasks or achieve your goals?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I still get everything done that I need to do. I finished my graduate degree years ago, and I always get my lesson plans and grading done on time for my classes. \n\nI might be, you know, shifting around in my chair or pacing around the living room while I do those things, but the work still gets finished. So, no, it hasn't really stopped me from completing my tasks or reaching my goals. I just might be a bit restless while I'm doing them.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been unable to participate in social activities or occupational tasks because of your need to be active and move?\n\nPatient: No, it's never actually stopped me from participating in anything. Um, I still go to everything I'm supposed to. Like I mentioned, I still attend my staff meetings and my book club, and I obviously teach my classes every day. So, no, I've never had to skip an activity or a work task just because I feel restless. I just go ahead and do it, even if I have to shift around while I'm there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to constantly be active and move is affecting your performance or relationships at work in a major way?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it is affecting my relationships, actually. They get annoyed with all my shifting and pacing. I can walk up and down the aisles, write on the board, and check on the students, so it's not out of the ordinary to be active. \n\nSo, no, it hasn't impacted my ability to function effectively with the kids or do my daily work. It's really just those times when I'm expected to sit quietly, like the staff meetings I mentioned, where it becomes an issue. But the teaching itself is fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to constantly be active and move is currently causing problems for you in more than one area of your life, such as at work and in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'd say it is. People get annoyed with me, and instead of feeling connected to my friends or coworkers, I just end up leaving those situations feeling really embarrassed.
